A parametrized Pontryagin–Thom theorem Thanks: DA was supported by the National Science Foundation under award 1945639. JF was supported by the National Science Foundation under award 1812057. This material is based upon work supported by the National Science Foundation under award 1928930 while the authors participated in a program supported by the Mathematical Sciences Research Institute during the Summer of 2022 in partnership with the Universidad Nacional Autonoma de Mexico.
David Ayala & John Francis

Abstract
. We prove a space-level enhancement of the Pontryagin–Thom theorem, identifying the space of maps from a manifold to a Thom space with a moduli space of submanifolds.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 57R90. Secondary 57N75, 55U10, 58D29.

1.
Introduction

Let
M
be an
(n+k)
-dimensional manifold with boundary.
Suppose
M
is finitary, which is to say it may be identified as a compact manifold with corners remove some faces.
Let
\xi=(E\to B)
be a rank-
k
vector bundle, with associated Thom space
\Th(\xi)
. The Pontryagin–Thom theorem identifies the set of homotopy classes of pointed maps from the one-point compactification
M^{+}
to
\Th(\xi)
with a set of embedded cobordism classes of codimension-
k
submanifolds of
M
.

This paper answers the following question.

Question.

Is there a space-level refinement of the Pontryagin–Thom theorem, in which the space of pointed maps
\Map_{\ast}(M^{+},\Th(\xi))
is homotopy equivalent to a moduli space of submanifolds of
M
?

This moduli space of submanifolds involves the following topological data: A
codimension-
\xi
submanifold
of
M
is a codimension-
k
submanifold
W\subset M
with boundary
\partial W=W\cap\partial M
such that
W
is transverse to the boundary
\partial M
, which is furthermore equipped with a map
g\colon W\rightarrow B
and an isomorphism between vector bundles over
W

\alpha\colon\nu_{W\subset M}\xrightarrow{~\cong~}g^{\ast}\xi

from the normal bundle of
W\subset M
to the pullback of
\xi
along
g
.
For
(W_{0},g_{0},\alpha_{0})
and
(W_{1},g_{1},\alpha_{1})
compact codimension-
\xi
submanifolds of
M
, an
embedded cobordism
from the first to the second is a compact codimension-
\xi
submanifold
(W,g,\alpha)
of
[0,1]\times M
such that, for each
i=0,1
, there are identities
W_{i}=W\cap(\{i\}\times M)
and
g_{i}=g_{|W_{i}}
and
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{|W_{i}}
.

Our answer to the main question involves, for each
p\geq 0
, the space
\Bord^{\xi}_{p}(M)
of compact codimension-
\xi
submanifolds of
\Delta^{p}\times M
,
organized as a simplicial topological space

\Bord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)

in a way introduced by Quinn
[
Qu1
]
.
In particular,
\Bord^{\xi}_{0}(M)
is a space of compact codimension-
\xi
submanifolds of
M
, and
\Bord^{\xi}_{1}(M)
is a space of codimension-
\xi
embedded cobordisms between such.
Forgetting the topology on the topological spaces
\Bord^{\xi}_{p}(M)
defines a simplicial set
\dBord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)
.

The following is the main result of this paper.
For a
X
a pointed space, let
\cMap(M,X)
denote the subspace of
\Map(M,X)
consisting of the
compactly-supported
maps (i.e., maps
f:M\rightarrow X
that carry the complement of a compact subspace to the basepoint of
X
).

Theorem 1.0.1

(Parametrized Pontryagin–Thom)
.

Let
\xi
be a vector bundle of rank
k
.
Let
M
be a smooth manifold with boundary.
There are equivalences between spaces,

\bigl|\dBord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)\bigr|~\simeq~\bigl|\Bord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)\bigr|~\simeq~{\sf cMap}\bigl(M,\Th(\xi)\bigr)~,

which are covariantly functorial with respect to open embeddings in
M
.
Furthermore, the simplicial set
\dBord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)
is a Kan complex, and the simplicial space
\Bord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)
satisfies the Kan condition.

This result is closely related to a number of previous works, which we discuss in §
1.2
. The principal new idea we introduce is a simplicial space of transverse maps, so as to interpolate between the mapping space and the moduli space of submanifolds:

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\cMap^{\pitchfork}_{\bullet}\bigl(M,\Th(\xi)\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bf\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec.transv}}
\scriptstyle{\bf\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec.PT}}
\textstyle{\cMap_{\bullet}\bigl(M,\Th(\xi)\bigr)}
\textstyle{\Bord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)~.}

In
§
2
, we establish a parametrized transversality theorem (Theorem
2.5.1
), which states that the inclusion of transverse maps into all maps induces an equivalence on geometric realizations:

\bigl|\cMap^{\pitchfork}_{\bullet}\bigl(M,\Th(\xi)\bigr)\bigr|~\simeq~\cMap\bigl(M,\Th(\xi)\bigr)~.

The proof relies on extension properties of smooth transverse maps (Lemmas
2.3.4
and
2.3.3
), similar to the Whitney extension theorem
[
Wh
]
.

In
§
4
, we prove that the assignment sending a map
f\colon M\rightarrow\Th(\xi)
to the preimage
f^{-1}(B)\subset M
of the zero-section
B\subset\Th(\xi)
defines a levelwise equivalence between simplicial spaces,

(-)^{-1}(B)\colon\cMap^{\pitchfork}_{\bullet}\bigl(M,\Th(\xi)\bigr)\longrightarrow\Bord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)~,

where the mapping space is endowed with the compact-open
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}
topology, and the space of submanifolds is topologized as a quotient of embedding spaces
\Emb(W,M)
indexed by diffeomorphism classes of
W
. In particular the assignment
(-)^{-1}(B)
is
continuous
with respect to these topologies, which is the most technical result in this paper (Lemma
4.1.2
). The techniques involved are similar to Palais’s proof of the parametrized isotopy extension theorem
[
Pa
]
. Once this continuity is established, then the proof of levelwise equivalence is a straightforward implementation of Pontryagin–Thom theory in compactly parameter families, organized by simplicial homotopy theory:
parametrized collapse maps (Theorem
4.3.2
) define a homotopy inverse to the zero-section preimage map.

In order to implement the above arguments of §
4
, the preceding
§
3
constructs the simplicial space
\Bord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)
. The key technical result is Lemma
3.5.1
which coherently matches normal bundles along the simplicial structure maps of
\Bord^{k}_{\bullet}(C)
; its proof occupies §
3.6
. The presentation of
\Bord^{k}_{\bullet}(M)
as a simplicial topological space allows for the construction of the map in Lemma
4.4.1
and the map (
54
).

In
§
5
, we construct a simplicial set
\dBord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)
, which we prove is a Kan complex. We construct a natural map
\dBord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)\rightarrow\Bord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)
and prove that it is a trivial Kan fibration (Corollary
5.3.4
), which is a slight weakening of the condition that a simplicial topological space
\tBord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)
is Reedy fibrant (see Remark
5.3.5
). This implies that their geometric realizations are equivalent, and so
\bigl|\dBord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)\bigr|
also models the mapping space
\cMap(M,\Th(\xi))
.

Remark 1.0.2
.

Although we have defined
\Bord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)
as a simplicial space, we think of it as an
(\infty,\infty)
-category, in which the
r
-morphisms are
(n+r)
-dimensional submanifolds of
\Delta^{r}\times M
. Consequently, this putative
(\oo,\oo)
-category has noninvertible
r
-morphisms for all
r>0
.
In the work
[
GH
]
,
(\infty,\infty)
-categories are presented as presheaves on a category
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}
that satisfy certain descent conditions.
The category
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}
is the
oriental category
– it is gaunt, locally finite, and it admits a monomorphism
\mathbf{\Delta}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}
from the simplex category that is an equivalence between sets of objects.
We expect the simplicial space
\Bord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)\in\PShv(\mathbf{\Delta})
naturally extends as a presheaf on
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}
and thereby presents an
(\infty,\infty)
-category.
1
1

1

Our expectation is founded on observing a natural extension of the functor
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}\xrightarrow{\Delta^{\bullet}}\cBun
, in the sense of
[
AFR
]
, which selects the skeletally stratified simplices, to
\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}^{\op}\to\cBun
.

The work
[
GH
]
does not impose univalence in their notion of of an
(\infty,\infty)
-category, and we likewise expect that the existence of non-trivial embedded h-cobordisms to mean that the
(\infty,\infty)
-category
\Bord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)
will not be univalent complete.
Fulfilling these expectations would be an interesting project, which we pose for an interested reader.
Premised on these expectation, we further expect the
(\infty,\infty)
-category presented by
\Bord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)
to be a sequential colimit of the finite tangle
(\oo,r)
-categories defined in a standard way (e.g., in terms of
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{r}
), at least in the case that
B\xrightarrow{\xi}\BO(k)
is base-changed from a stable vector bundle
\widetilde{B}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\xi}}\BO
along
\BO(k)\to\BO
:

\colim\Bigl(\Bord^{\zeta_{0}}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\Bord^{\zeta_{1}}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\dots\to\Bord^{\zeta_{r}}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\cdots\Bigr)~\simeq~\Bord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{R}^{k})~,

where each
\zeta_{r}
is the base-change of
\widetilde{B}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\xi}}\BO
along
\BO(r)\to\BO\xrightarrow{-1}\BO
.

Remark 1.0.3
.

For
\xi
a smooth vector bundle over an infinite-dimensional manifold, we expect the simplicial space
\Bord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)
to have a refinement as a simplicial
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}
-sheaf. (See Remark
3.1.12
.)
Such a lift of
\Bord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)
might be interesting in its own right; it might also simplify several arguments in this work, notably around the repeated use of smooth approximation.

1.1.
Classical Pontryagin–Thom from parametrized Pontryagin–Thom

Applying
\pi_{0}
to the equivalence of Theorem
1.0.1
recovers the following form of the classical Pontryagin–Thom theorem (e.g., see Proposition 4.1 of
[
BRS
]
):

Theorem 1.1.1

(Pontryagin–Thom)
.

Let
M
be a finitary smooth manifold with boundary, and let
\xi
be a rank-
k
vector bundle over
B
.
There is a bijection between the set of embedded cobordism classes of compact codimension-
\xi
compact submanifolds of
M
with boundary and the set of pointed homotopy classes of pointed maps to the Thom space of
\xi
:

\Bigl\{W\underset{\rm codim{\text{-}}\xi}{\subset}M\Bigl\}_{/\rm Emb~Cob}~\cong~[M^{+},\Th(\xi)]~.

Proof of Theorem
1.1.1
from Theorem
1.0.1
.

Recall that, for
X_{\bullet}
a simplicial space, there is a canonical bijection between sets

\pi_{0}|X_{\bullet}|~\cong~(\pi_{0}X_{0})_{/\sim}~,

where
\sim
is the equivalence relation generated by declaring the faces of each edge to be equivalent:
[d_{0}e]\sim[d_{1}e]
for each
e\in X_{1}
.
In the case of the simplicial space
\Bord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)
, we identify the space of 0-simplices as a space of compact codimenion-
\xi
submanifolds of
M
with boundary.
So
\pi_{0}\Bord^{\xi}_{0}(M)
is the set of compact codimension-
\xi
submanifolds of
M
with boundary, up to isotopy between such.
Similarly,
\Bord^{\xi}_{1}(M)
is a space of compact codimension-
\xi
submanifolds of
[0,1]\times M
with corners.
For
i=0,1
, the
i^{th}
face map
\Bord^{\xi}_{1}(M)\xrightarrow{d_{i}}\Bord^{\xi}_{0}(M)
is given by
[0,1]\times M\supset W\mapsto W\cap(\{i\}\times M)\subset M
.
Consequently, we identify the set of path-components

\pi_{0}|\Bord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)|~\cong~\left\{W\subset M\right\}_{/\rm Emb~Cob}

as the set of embedded cobordism classes of compact codimension-
\xi
submanifolds of
M
.
The result follows.
∎

The stable Pontryagin–Thom theorem has been particularly central in algebraic topology.
Let
\boldsymbol{\xi}
denote a
stable
vector bundle, by which we mean the following data:

•

a sequence of maps
B_{0}\xrightarrow{i_{0}}B_{1}\xrightarrow{i_{1}}B_{2}\xrightarrow{i_{2}}\cdots\rightarrow B=\varinjlim B_{k}
;

•

a rank-
k
vector bundle
\xi_{k}=(E_{k}\to B_{k})
, for each
k\geq 0
;

•

an isomorphism between vector bundles
\xi_{k}\oplus\epsilon^{1}_{B_{k}}\cong i_{k}^{\ast}\xi_{k+1}
over
B_{k}
, for each
k\geq 0
.

These data determine, for each
k\geq 0
, a pointed map

\Sigma\Th(\xi_{k})\simeq\Th(\xi_{k}\oplus\epsilon^{1}_{B_{k}})\longrightarrow\Th(\xi_{k+1})~.

The
Thom spectrum
of
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is the spectrum
\Th(\boldsymbol{\xi})
associated to this sequence of pointed maps.
The stabilization of the classical Pontryagin–Thom theorem can be formulated in the following terms: a stably
\boldsymbol{\xi}
-framed compact
n
-manifold with boundary over
M
consists of:

•

a compact
n
-manifold with boundary
W^{n}
;

•

a map
W^{n}\xrightarrow{g}B
;

•

a smooth map
W^{n}\xrightarrow{f}M
transverse to
\partial M
and for which
\partial W=f^{-1}(\partial M)
; and

•

an isomorphism
f^{\ast}\tau_{M}\cong\tau_{W}\oplus g^{\ast}\boldsymbol{\xi}
between stable vector bundles.

Taking the direct limit of the Pontryagin–Thom theorem for
M\times\mathbb{R}^{r}
, as
r\mapsto\infty
, implies the following (e.g., see Theorem 5.1 of
[
BRS
]
).

Theorem 1.1.2

(Stable Pontryagin–Thom)
.

Let
n,k\geq 0
.
Let
M
be a finitary smooth
(n+k)
-manifold with boundary, and let
\boldsymbol{\xi}
be as above.
There is a bijection between the set of stably
\boldsymbol{\xi}
-framed cobordism classes of stably
\boldsymbol{\xi}
-framed compact
n
-manifolds with boundary over
M
and the
k^{th}
reduced cohomology of
M^{+}
with coefficients in the Thom spectrum
\Th(\boldsymbol{\xi})
:

\Bigl\{W^{n}\to M\Bigr\}_{/\rm Cob}~\cong~\widetilde{\mathsf{H}}^{k}\bigl(M^{+},\Th(\boldsymbol{\xi})\bigr)~.

Stabilizing Theorem
1.0.1
gives a parametrized version of the stable Pontryagin–Thom theorem.
For each choice of
k\geq 0
and
M
, the data
\boldsymbol{\xi}
determine a sequence of morphisms between simplicial spaces

\Bord^{\xi_{k}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{0})\to\Bord^{\xi_{k+1}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{1})\to\Bord^{\xi_{k+2}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\cdots~,

as well as a sequence of morphisms between simplicial sets
\dBord^{\xi_{k}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{0})\to\dBord^{\xi_{k+1}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{1})\to\cdots
,
which are implemented by the inclusions

\Delta^{\bullet}\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{r}=\Delta^{\bullet}\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\{0\}~\hookrightarrow~\Delta^{\bullet}\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}=\Delta^{\bullet}\times M\times\mathbb{R}^{r+1}~.

Denote the colimit simplicial space

\Bord^{\Sigma^{k}\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\bullet}(M)~:=~\underset{r\geq 0}{\colim}\Bord^{\xi_{k+r}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{r})~,

and the colimit simplicial set
\dBord^{\Sigma^{k}\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\bullet}(M):=\underset{r\geq 0}{\colim}\Bord^{\xi_{k+r}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{r})
.
Evidently, for each
r\geq 0
, framed embeddings among finite disjoint unions of
\mathbb{R}^{r}
defines an
\mathcal{E}_{r}
-algebra structure on the simplicial space
\Bord^{\xi_{k+r}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{r})
.
As detailed in §4.1 of
[
AF
]
, these structures determine the structure of an
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
-algebra on the simplicial space
\Bord^{\Sigma^{k}\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\bullet}(M)
.
Denote the
k^{th}
space of the Thom spectrum

\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{k}\Th(\boldsymbol{\xi}):=\underset{r\geq 0}{\colim}\ \Omega^{r}\Th(\xi_{k+r})~,

which is an
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
-algebra in
\Spaces
.

Corollary 1.1.3

(Parametrized stable Pontryagin–Thom)
.

Let
M
be a finitary manifold with boundary, and let
\boldsymbol{\xi}
be a stable vector bundle.
For each
k\geq 0
, there are equivalences between spaces,

\bigl|\dBord^{\Sigma^{k}\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\bullet}(M)\bigr|~\simeq~\bigl|\Bord^{\Sigma^{k}\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\bullet}(M)\bigr|~\simeq~\Map_{\ast}\bigl(M^{+},\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{k}\Th(\boldsymbol{\xi})\bigr)~,

the second of which is one between
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
-algebras.

The stable Pontryagin–Thom Theorem
1.1.2
follows from Corollary
1.1.3
by applying path-components.

Remark 1.1.4
.

Corollary
1.1.3
gives a Kan complex of stably-framed iterated cobordisms whose geometric realization is
\Omega^{\infty}\mathbb{S}
, the zeroth space of the sphere spectrum.
That is, in the case that
\boldsymbol{\xi}=\fr
codifies stable normal framings (i.e.,
B_{k}=\ast
for all
k\geq 0
), Corollary
1.1.3
gives a Kan complex
\dBord^{\fr}_{\bullet}(M)
of iterated stably-framed cobordisms whose geometric realization
|\dBord^{\fr}_{\bullet}(M)|\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\mathbb{S}
is equivalent with the zeroth space of the sphere spectrum.

Proof of Corollary
1.1.3
from Theorem
1.0.1
.

For each
r\geq 0
, the morphism between simplicial sets
\dBord^{\xi_{k+r}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{r})\to\dBord^{\xi_{k+r+1}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{r+1})
is evidently a monomorphism.
Consequently, the canonical map between spaces
\underset{r\geq 0}{\colim}|\dBord^{\xi_{k+r}}_{\bullet}(M)|\to|\dBord^{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\bullet}(M)|
is an equivalence.
Meanwhile, the canonical map between spaces
\underset{r\geq 0}{\colim}|\Bord^{\xi_{k+r}}_{\bullet}(M)|\to|\Bord^{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\bullet}(M)|
is an equivalence because the functor
\Fun(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op},\Spaces)\xrightarrow{|-|}\Spaces
commutes with colimits.
In this way, the first equivalence follows from the first equivalence of Theorem
1.0.1
, by taking sequential colimits.

The second equivalence is a composition of the following sequence of routine equivalences between spaces:

\displaystyle\bigl|\Bord^{\Sigma^{k}\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{\bullet}(M)\bigr|

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\underset{r\geq 0}{\colim}\bigl|\Bord^{\xi_{k+r}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{r})\bigr|

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\underset{r\geq 0}{\colim}\cMap\bigl(M\times\mathbb{R}^{r},\Th(\xi_{k+r})\bigr)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\underset{r\geq 0}{\colim}\Map_{\ast}\bigl((M\times\mathbb{R}^{r})^{+},\Th(\xi_{k+r})\bigr)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\underset{r\geq 0}{\colim}\Map_{\ast}\bigl(\Sigma^{r}M^{+},\Th(\xi_{k+r})\bigr)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\underset{r\geq 0}{\colim}\Map_{\ast}\bigl(M^{+},\Omega^{r}\Th(\xi_{k+r})\bigr)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\Map_{\ast}\Bigl(M^{+},\underset{r\geq 0}{\colim}\ \Omega^{r}\Th(\xi_{k+r})\Bigr)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\Map_{\ast}\bigl(M^{+},\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{k}\Th(\boldsymbol{\xi})\bigr)~.

The first equivalence follows by intercommutation of colimits: in particular, geometric realizations commute with direct limits. The second equivalence is Theorem
1.0.1
applied to
M\times\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
\xi_{k+r}
.
For the third equivalence, since
M\times\mathbb{R}^{r}
is locally compact, there is a canonical map

(1)

\cMap(M\times\mathbb{R}^{r},\Th(\xi))\longrightarrow\Map_{\ast}((M\times\mathbb{R}^{r})^{+},\Th(\xi))~.

We postpone to the next paragraph a proof that (
1
) is an equivalence. The fourth equivalence follows since the one-point compactification of the product is equivalent to the smash product of the one-point compactifications:
(M\times\mathbb{R}^{r})^{+}\simeq M^{+}\wedge(\mathbb{R}^{r})^{+}\simeq\Sigma^{r}M^{+}
.
The fifth equivalence is the suspension-loop adjunction. The penultimate equivalence again uses that
M
is finitary, so that
M^{+}
is a compact object in the
\infty
-category
\Spaces
. The last equivalence is definitional.

We now prove (
1
) is an equivalence.
Consider the poset
{\sf Cpt}(M)
of compact subspaces of
M
ordered by inclusion.
Recognize the subspace

\Map(M,\Th(\xi))~\supset~\cMap(M,\Th(\xi))~\simeq~\underset{K\in{\sf Cpt}(M)}{\colim}\Map_{K}(M,\Th(\xi))

as a colimit, where
\Map_{K}(M,\Th(\xi))\subset\cMap(M,\Th(\xi))
is the subspace of consisting of those smooth compactly-supported maps
f\colon M\rightarrow\Th(\xi)
such that
{\sf Supp}(f):=\overline{f^{-1}(E)}\subset K
.
Consequently, the map (
1
) may be implemented as precomposition by the
{\sf Cpt}(M)
-indexed system of collapse-maps

M^{+}\longrightarrow M\underset{M\smallsetminus C}{\amalg}\ast~.

Next, using that
M
is finitary, choose a compact manifold
\overline{M}
with corners such that
M
is identified as
\overline{M}
remove some faces.
Denote
\partial M:=\overline{M}\smallsetminus M
.
Consider the full subposet
\mathsf{C}\subset{\sf Open}(\overline{M})
consisting of the collar-neighborhoods of
\partial M\subset\overline{M}
.
This collection
\mathsf{C}
of neighborhoods is a basis for the topology about
\partial M\subset\overline{M}
.
In particular,
\mathsf{C}
is cofiltered, and the functor between posets
\mathsf{C}^{\op}\xrightarrow{C\mapsto M\smallsetminus C}{\sf Cpt}(M)
is final, as a functor between
\infty
-categories.
In particular, we have a canonical identification between spaces:

\underset{C\in\mathsf{C}^{\op}}{\colim}\Map_{M\smallsetminus C}(M,\Th(\xi))\xrightarrow{~\simeq~}\underset{K\in{\sf Cpt}(M)}{\colim}\Map_{K}(M,\Th(\xi))~\simeq~\cMap(M,\Th(\xi))~.

Now, for
C\in\mathsf{C}
a collar-neighborhood of
\partial M\subset\overline{M}
, a choice of diffeomorphism
C\cong\partial M\times[0,1)
reveals that the collapse-map
M^{+}\to M\underset{M\smallsetminus C}{\amalg}\ast
implements an equivalence between spaces:

\Map_{M\smallsetminus C}(M,\Th(\xi))\xrightarrow{~\simeq~}\Map_{\ast}(M^{+},\Th(\xi))~.

Using that
\mathsf{C}^{\op}
is filtered, this implies the canonical map

\Map_{\ast}(M^{+},\Th(\xi))\xrightarrow{~\simeq~}\underset{C^{\circ}\in\mathsf{C}^{\op}}{\colim}\Map_{M\smallsetminus C}(M,\Th(\xi))

is an equivalence.
We conclude that the map (
1
) is an equivalence, as desired.

For each
r\geq 0
, both
\Bord^{\xi_{r}}_{\bullet}(N)
and
\Map_{\ast}\left(N^{+},\Th(\xi_{r})\right)
are functorial among open embeddings in the argument
N
, carrying disjoint unions to products and isotopy equivalences to equivalences.
Therefore, both
\Bord^{\xi_{r}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{r})
and
\Map_{\ast}\left((M\times\mathbb{R}^{r})^{+},\Th(\xi_{r})\right)\simeq\Map_{\ast}\left(M^{+},\Omega^{r}\Th(\xi_{r})\right)
have canonical structures of
\mathcal{E}_{r}
-algebras.
The naturality of Theorem
1.0.1
therefore implies its equivalence between spaces
\left|\Bord^{\xi_{r}}_{\bullet}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{r})\right|\simeq\Map_{\ast}\left(M^{+},\Omega^{r}\Th(\xi_{r})\right)
lifts as one between
\mathcal{E}_{r}
-algebras.
Consequently, the second equivalence is an equivalence of
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
-algebras.

∎

1.2.
Comparison to preceding works

Our parametrized Pontryagin–Thom theorem is distinct from the enhancements of Pontryagin–Thom of Galatius–Madsen–Tillmann–Weiss
[
GMTW
]
, Ayala
[
Ay
]
, Randal-Williams
[
RW1
]
, and Schommer-Pries
[
SP
]
in the following two ways.
First, while both we and they recover the set of cobordism classes of
n
-manifolds as
\pi_{0}
of a classifying space, these spaces are substantially different.
We expect our space is the classifying space of an
(\oo,\oo)
-category (see Remark
1.0.2
), in which the
r
-morphisms are
(n+r)
-dimensional manifolds with corners.
For
[
GMTW
]
,
[
Ay
]
,
[
RW1
]
,
[
SP
]
, the space is a classifying space of an
(\oo,m)
-category for necessarily finite
m
: its objects are
n
-manifolds, and its
k
-morphisms are
(k-1)
-parameter families of diffeomorphisms for
k\geq m
.
Second, those works employ Graeme Segal’s technique of
scanning maps
(as in
[
Se
]
,
[
Mc1
]
,
[
Mc2
]
), and consequently are compatible with
tangential
structures on the manifold
M
. Our method uses the more classical Pontryagin–Thom collapse maps, and hence is compatible with
normal
structures. Stably these are the same (hence both our theorems imply the stable Pontryagin–Thom theorem), but unstably they are distinct and thus yield different unstable parametrized versions of the Pontryagin–Thom theorem.

The essential idea of building a simplicial set (or at least semi-simplicial) whose
p
-simplices are codimension-
k
submanifolds with corners in
\Delta^{p}
dates at least to Quinn’s thesis: See §1.4
[
Qu1
]
, as well as a discussion of this history by Laures–McClure in §1.1
[
LM
]
. In particular, Quinn
[
Qu2
]
constructed a semi-simplicial set (i.e., a
\Delta
-set) similar to the stabilization of
\dBord_{\bullet}(M)
in the argument
M
, in the case in which
\xi
is the universal bundle over
\BSO
. Using the Pontryagin–Thom theorem, Quinn deduces that the homotopy groups of the realization of this semi-simplicial set are the bordism homology groups of
M
, a result closely related to Theorem 5.1 of Buoncristiano–Rourke–Sanderson
[
BRS
]
.

In the setting of topological manifolds, Laures–McClure
[
LM
]
and Randal-Williams
[
RW2
]
both consider bordism-type models for the Thom spectrum of
\Top
. In Appendix B
[
LM
]
, prove a topological version of Quinn’s theorem, that
{\sf MTop}
is the realization of a Quinn-type semi-simplicial set. Randal-Williams constructs a semi-simplicial set
{\sf Mock}(d,n)
which is a topological analogue of our
\dBord_{\bullet}^{\gamma_{n-d}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
\gamma_{n-d}
is the universal bundle over
\BSO(n-d)
.
In §4.3
[
RW2
]
, he outlines how, in the large
n
limit, topological transversality would prove an equivalence between the realization
|{\sf Mock}(d)|
and the space
\Omega^{\oo+d}{\sf MSTop}=\Omega^{\infty+d}\Th(\boldsymbol{\gamma})
.

Both Quinn and Randal-Williams include a
collaring
condition in the definition of their semi-simplicial sets of manifolds, which is convenient for verifying the Kan condition. In block automorphism spaces of manifolds, the inclusion of this collaring condition is standard (see
[
BLR
]
, §1.3 of
[
Kr
]
, Definition 2.2.1 of
[
HLLR
]
) because it makes the Kan condition straightforward to establish. The downside is that it complicates the definition of degeneracies, resulting in only semi-simplicial sets, rather than simplicial sets.
However, this collaring condition is not expected to affect the homotopy type: See Theorem 2.60
[
GIMM
]
. Like in
[
GIMM
]
, the present work does not include a collaring condition in our definition of the spaces
\Bord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)
, which allows us to construct simplicial, rather than merely semi-simplicial, objects. We nevertheless verify the Kan condition for the simplicial space
\Bord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)
as well as the simplicial set
\dBord_{\bullet}^{\xi}(M)
.

Similarly, in the study of smooth singular complexes, it is also common to include a collaring condition. See, for example,
[
Ki
]
and
[
CW
]
. See Remark 1.7 and Warning 2.24 of
[
OT
]
for a discussion of diffeologies on simplices and the effect on the Kan condition for smooth singular complexes.
Similar to our definition of
\Bord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)
and
\dBord^{\xi}_{\bullet}(M)
, we do not impose a collaring condition in the definition of the simplicial smooth mapping spaces
\Map^{\sm}_{\bullet}(M,\Th(\xi))
and
\Map^{\pitchfork}_{\bullet}(M,\Th(\xi))
, but still verify the Kan condition.

Terminology

•

The term
manifold
, and
manifold with boundary
, refers to a smooth manifold, and a smooth manifold with boundary, respectively.
The term
manifold with corners
refers to a smooth manifold with corners in the sense of Definition 2.1 of
[
Jo
]
.
For
M
an
n
-manifold with corners, its underlying topological space is canonically stratified by the poset
[n]=\{0<\dots<n\}
, such that, for
i\in[n]
, the (locally closed)
i
-stratum is the locus of those
x\in M
for which there exists an open embedding
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{i}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-i}\xrightarrow{\varphi}M
such that
\varphi(0)=x
.
For
i\in[n]
, an
i
-
face
is a connected component
F\subset M
of the
i
-stratum.

•

A
space
is an object in the
\oo
-category of spaces,
\Spaces
. A
topological space
is an object of the ordinary category of topological spaces,
\Top
. There is a functor

\Top\rightarrow\Spaces

which associates to a topological space its underlying space. We will use the same notation for both a topological space and its underlying space. (E.g.,
\Sub^{\xi}(M)
is defined as a space in Definition
3.4.3
and as a topological space through Proposition
5.2.1
.)

•

For
\chi=(E\to B)
a vector bundle, we often denote its total space as
E(\chi):=E
and its base as
B(\chi):=B
.

2.
Parametrized transversality

Throughout this section, we fix the following.

M
is a manifold with boundary.

\xi=(E\to B)
is a smooth rank-
k
vector bundle.

C
is a manifold with corners.

X
is a pointed topological space; the complement
X\smallsetminus\ast
of the base point has the structure of a smooth manifold; and
X_{0}\subset X\smallsetminus\ast
is a properly embedded smooth submanifold whose closure in
X
does not contain the base point.

2.1.
Spaces of compactly-supported maps

Definition 2.1.1
.

A
compactly-supported map
from
C
to
X
is a map
f\colon C\rightarrow X
whose support
{\sf Supp}(f):=\overline{f^{-1}(X\smallsetminus\ast)}\subset C
is compact.
The topological space

\cMap(C,X)

is the set of compactly-supported maps from
C
to
X
, endowed with the compact-open topology.

Definition 2.1.2
.

A
smooth compactly-supported map
from
C
to
X
is a compactly-supported map
f\colon C\rightarrow X
such that the restriction
f_{|}\colon f^{-1}(X\smallsetminus\ast)\rightarrow X\smallsetminus\ast
is smooth.
The topological space of smooth compactly-supported maps from
C
to
X

\cMap^{\sm}(C,X)

is the set of smooth compactly-supported maps endowed with the topology generated by the collection of subsets

N(f,K,K^{\prime},(U,\varphi),(V,\psi),V^{\prime},\epsilon)~\subset~\cMap^{\sf sm}(C,X)~,

given as follows.

Let
\{(U,\varphi)\}
be a member of the atlas for
C
, and let
K\subset K^{\prime}\subset U
be concentric compact subspaces contained in
U
.
Let
\{(V,\psi)\}
be a member of the atlas for
X\smallsetminus\ast
, and let
V\subset V^{\prime}\subset X
be an open subset containing
V
.
Let
f\in\cMap^{\sm}(C,X)
such that
f(K^{\prime})\subset V^{\prime}
and
f(K)\subset V
.
Let
\epsilon>0
.
The subset
N(f,K,K^{\prime},(U,\varphi),(V,\psi),V^{\prime},\epsilon)
consists of those elements
g\in\cMap^{\sf sm}(C,X)
such that

–

g(K^{\prime})
is contained in
V^{\prime}
;

–

g(K)
is contained in
V
;

–

|\!|D^{(r)}_{x}(\psi f\varphi^{-1})-D^{(r)}_{x}(\psi g\varphi^{-1})|\!|<\epsilon
for all
x\in K
.

Remark 2.1.3
.

Given a choice of Riemannian metric on
X\smallsetminus\ast
, a sequence
f_{n}
in
\cMap^{\sf sm}(C,X)
converges to
f
if and only if: it converges uniformly on compact subspaces; for each
x\in M
such that
f(x)\neq\ast
, the derivatives
D^{(r)}f_{n}(x)
converge uniformly to
D^{(r)}f(x)
.

Definition 2.1.4
.

A
transverse compactly-supported map
from
C
to
X
is a smooth compactly-supported
f\colon C\rightarrow X
such that for each face
F\subset C
, the smooth map
f_{|}\colon F\cap f^{-1}(X\smallsetminus\ast)\rightarrow X\smallsetminus\ast
is transverse to
X_{0}
.
The topological subspace

\cMap^{\pitchfork}(C,X)~\subset~\cMap^{\sf sm}(C,X)

consists of the transverse compactly-supported maps.

2.2.
Simplicial spaces of compactly-supported maps

We recall some standard cosimplicial objects.
Let
p\geq 0
.
The
topological
p
-simplex
and the
extended
p
-simplex
are the respective subspaces:

\Delta^{p}~:=~\left\{\{0,\dots,p\}\xrightarrow{t}[0,1]\mid\underset{i\in\{0,\dots,p\}}{\sum}t_{i}=1\right\}~\subset~[0,1]^{\{0,\dots,p\}}~,

and

\Delta^{p}_{e}~:=~\left\{\{0,\dots,p\}\xrightarrow{t}\mathbb{R}\mid\underset{i\in\{0,\dots,p\}}{\sum}t_{i}=1\right\}~\subset~\mathbb{R}^{\{0,\dots,p\}}~.

Each morphism
[p]\xrightarrow{\sigma}[q]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
determines maps

\Delta^{p}\xrightarrow{~\sigma_{\ast}}~\Delta^{q}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Delta^{p}_{e}\xrightarrow{~\sigma_{\ast}~}\Delta^{q}_{e}

both given by

\sigma_{\ast}(t)\colon j\longmapsto\underset{i\in\sigma^{-1}(j)}{\sum}t_{i}~.

Observation 2.2.1
.

(1)

For each morphism
[p]\xrightarrow{\sigma}[q]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
, the map
\Delta^{p}_{e}\xrightarrow{\sigma_{\ast}}\Delta^{q}_{e}
is an affine map between affine subspaces of Euclidean spaces.
In particular,
\sigma_{\ast}
factors as a submersion followed by a proper embedding.

(2)

For each
p\geq 0
, the topological
p
-simplex inherits the structure of a
p
-manifold with corners, from the standard structure of a compact manifold with corners on finite-fold products of the closed interval.
The evident inclusion
\Delta^{p}\subset\Delta^{p}_{e}
witnesses a collar extension.

(3)

These data organize as a cosimplicial smooth manifold
\Delta^{\bullet}_{e}
and as a cosimplicial smooth manifold with corners
\Delta^{\bullet}
.

(4)

For each morphism
[p]\xrightarrow{\sigma}[q]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
, the map
\Delta^{p}\xrightarrow{\sigma_{\ast}}\Delta^{q}
is proper and it restricts to each face of of
\Delta^{p}
as a submersion onto the face containing its image.

Observation
2.2.1
implies the following.

Observation 2.2.2
.

Let
[p]\xrightarrow{\sigma}[q]
be a morphism in
\mathbf{\Delta}
.

(1)

The map
\Delta^{p}\times M\xrightarrow{\sigma_{\ast}\times M}\Delta^{q}\times M
is a proper smooth map between manifolds with corners.

(2)

Let
\Delta^{q}\times M\xrightarrow{f}X
be a smooth map.
If
f
is transverse to
X_{0}\subset X
, then the composite map
f\circ(\sigma_{\ast}\times M)\colon\Delta^{p}\times M\xrightarrow{\sigma_{\ast}\times M}\Delta^{q}\times M\xrightarrow{f}X
is transverse to
X_{0}
.

Observation
2.2.2
allows us to define the simplicial structure maps in the following.

Definition 2.2.3
.

We denote simplicial topological spaces

\displaystyle{\cMap}_{\bullet}(M,X)

\displaystyle~:=~

\displaystyle\cMap(\Delta^{\bullet}\times M,X)~,

\displaystyle{\cMap}_{\bullet}^{\sm}(M,X)

\displaystyle~:=~

\displaystyle\cMap^{\sf sm}(\Delta^{\bullet}\times M,X)~,

\displaystyle{\cMap}_{\bullet}^{\pitchfork}(M,X)

\displaystyle~:=~

\displaystyle\cMap^{\pitchfork}(\Delta^{\bullet}\times M,X)~.

Observation 2.2.4
.

There are canonical injective morphisms between simplicial topological spaces

(2)

\cMap^{\pitchfork}_{\bullet}(M,X)\longrightarrow\cMap^{\sf sm}_{\bullet}(M,X)\longrightarrow\cMap_{\bullet}(M,X)\longleftarrow\cMap(M,X)~,

in which the domain of the last leftward morphism is the constant simplicial topological space, and it is induced by the unique natural transformation
\Delta^{\bullet}\to\ast
, which is by proper maps.

2.3.
Smooth extension and approximation results

In this technical subsection, we prove a few results about extending smooth maps respecting properness and transversality.

Definition 2.3.1
.

Let
C
be a manifold with corners.
A closed union of faces,
C_{0}\subset C
, is
properly acyclic
if it satisfies the following condition.

Let
C_{0}\subset V\subset C
be an open neighborhood of
C_{0}
whose complement
C\smallsetminus V\subset C
is compact.
There exists a smooth proper map
C\xrightarrow{e}C
with the following properties.

(1)

It is a diffeomorphism onto its image, which is open.

(2)

There is containment of its image
e(C)\subset V
.

(3)

It extends the inclusion
C_{0}\hookrightarrow C
.

We will be particularly concerned with the following example.

Example 2.3.2
.

Let
0\leq i\leq p
.
The closed union of faces
\Lambda^{p}_{i}\times M\subset\Delta^{p}\times M
is properly acyclic.

Lemma 2.3.3
.

Consider a solid diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}

in which
T
is a manifold,
C
is a manifold with corners,
C_{0}\subset C
is a closed union of faces, and
f
is a map whose restriction to each face in
C_{0}
is smooth.

(1)

If
T
is a vector space, then there is a smooth extension
\widetilde{f}
.

(2)

If
f
is proper and
T
is a vector space whose dimension is greater than that of
C
, then there is a proper smooth extension
\widetilde{f}
.

(3)

If
C_{0}\subset C
is properly acyclic,
then there is a smooth extension
\widetilde{f}
.

Proof.

We first prove statement (1).
Using that
C_{0}\subset C
is a closed union of faces, for each
x\in C_{0}
choose the following data:

•

Non-negative integers
\ell\geq 0
and
d\geq 0
.

•

A down-closed subset
I\subset\mathcal{P}(\{1,\dots,d\})
of the power set.

•

A smooth open embedding

\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\xrightarrow{~\varphi_{x}~}C

such that
\varphi_{x}(0)=x
and
\varphi_{x}^{-1}(C_{0})=\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times B(I)
.
Here,

B(I)~:=~\Bigl\{(r_{1},\dots,r_{d})\big|\ \{i\mid r_{i}\neq 0\}\in I\Bigr\}=\bigcup_{S\in I}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{S}~\subset~\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}

is the union of the coordinate planes, where
\mathbb{R}^{S}
is identified with the subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose
i^{th}
coordinate is zero for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\smallsetminus S
.

Define the extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times B(I)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{x}}_{|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\iota\circ f\circ\varphi_{x}}_{|}}

by the expression

\widetilde{f\circ{\varphi_{x}}_{|}}~:=~\sum_{S\in I}(-1)^{d-|S|+1}f\circ{\varphi_{x}}_{|}\circ(\id_{\mathbb{R}^{\ell}}\times{\sf proj}_{S})

where, for
S\in I
, the map
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\xrightarrow{{\sf proj}_{S}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{S}
is orthogonal projection onto the subspace non-negatively spanned by the
S
-coordinates.
Note that this expression is defined in terms of the vector space structure on
T
.
It is easily checked that the map
\widetilde{f\circ{\varphi_{x}}_{|}}
is smooth, and that signs cancel to ensure that it extends
f\circ{\varphi_{x}}_{|}
.
Denote the open neighborhood
x\in U_{x}\subset C
that is the image of
\varphi_{x}
.
Denote the composite map

\widetilde{f}_{x}\colon U_{x}\xrightarrow{~\varphi_{x}^{-1}~}\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{d}\xrightarrow{~\widetilde{f\circ{\varphi_{x}}_{|}}~}T~.

This map
\widetilde{f}_{x}
is a composition of smooth maps, and it is therefore smooth.
Furthermore, the maps
\widetilde{f}_{x}
and
f
restrict identically along
U_{x}\cap C_{0}
.

Let
x\in C\smallsetminus C_{0}
.
Using that
C_{0}\subset C
is a closed subspace, for each
x\in C\smallsetminus C_{0}
choose an open neighborhood
x\in U_{x}\subset C
such that
U_{x}\cap C_{0}=\emptyset
.
Choose a smooth map
U_{x}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{f}_{x}}T
.

Next, choose a smooth partition of unity
(\rho_{x})_{x\in C}
subordinate to the open cover
(U_{x})_{x\in C}
.
The map

\widetilde{f}\colon C\longrightarrow T~,\qquad\widetilde{f}~:=~\sum_{x\in C}\rho_{x}\cdot\widetilde{f}_{x}~,

is defined, and smooth, and it extends
f
.
This completes the proof of statement (1).

We now prove statement (2).
Once and for all, choose a linear inner product on
T
.
Using statement (1), choose a smooth extension
C\xrightarrow{\widetilde{f}}T
.
By Sard’s Theorem, the assumed dimension bound implies
\widetilde{f}
is not surjective.
So choose
t_{0}\in T
that is not contained in the image of
f
.
Using that
f
is assumed proper, there exists
r>0
such that the open ball
\mathsf{B}_{2r}(t_{0})\subset T
is disjoint from the image of
f
.
Choose a diffeomorphism
\alpha\colon T\smallsetminus\{t_{0}\}\cong T\smallsetminus\overline{\mathsf{B}}_{r}(0)
from
T
remove
t_{0}
to
T
remove the closed disk of radius
r
about
t_{0}
, such that
\alpha(t)=t
for each
t\in T\smallsetminus\mathsf{B}_{2r}(t_{0})
.
Observe that the resulting map
C\xrightarrow{\alpha\circ\widetilde{f}}T
is well-defined, smooth, and proper, and that it extends
C_{0}\xrightarrow{f}T
.
Upon replacing
\widetilde{f}
with
\alpha\circ\widetilde{f}
, in what follows we assume the image of
\widetilde{f}
is disjoint from
\mathsf{B}_{r}(t_{0})
.

Choose a proper continuous filler of the diagram

\hbox to91.61pt{\vbox to55.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 45.80505pt\lower-24.55193pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -23.37 -14.21 L 16.59 9.95}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.85571}{0.51744}{-0.51744}{0.85571}{12.16312pt}{7.29742pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
}}{
}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.07567pt}{-8.6481pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

Choose an open neighborhood
C_{0}\subset U\subset C
that admits a retraction
U\xrightarrow{\rho}C_{0}
, which is a deformation retraction.
Homotope
d
as needed to assume the restriction
d_{|U}\colon U\xrightarrow{\rho}C_{0}\xrightarrow{\lVert f-t_{0}\rVert}\mathbb{R}_{\geq r}
factors through
C_{0}
via the retraction.
Choose a smooth map
C\xrightarrow{\sigma}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
such that
\sigma(c)=c
for all
c\in C_{0}
and
\sigma\geq\frac{d}{\lVert\widetilde{f}-t_{0}\rVert}
– the assumption on
d_{|U}
ensures such a
\sigma
exists.
Consider the map

C\xrightarrow{~\widetilde{f}_{\sigma}}T~,\qquad c\longmapsto\sigma(c)\Bigl(\widetilde{f}(c)-t_{0}\Bigr)+t_{0}~,

which is evidently smooth.
For each
c\in C
, observe the inequality

\lVert\widetilde{f}_{\sigma}(c)-t_{0}\rVert~=~\sigma\lVert\widetilde{f}(c)-t_{0}\rVert~\geq~\frac{d(c)}{\lVert\widetilde{f}(c)-t_{0}\rVert}\lVert\widetilde{f}(c)-t_{0}\rVert~=~d(c)~.

Because
d
is proper, this inequality ensures
\widetilde{f}_{\sigma}
is also proper.
This completes the proof of statement (2).

We now prove statement (3).
Choose a proper smooth embedding
T\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathbb{R}^{N}
for some
N\geq 0
.
Choose a tubular neighborhood
\iota(T)\subset\widetilde{T}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and a smooth retraction
\widetilde{T}\xrightarrow{\pi}T
.
Using statement (1), choose a smooth extension
\widetilde{\iota\circ f}
of
\iota\circ f
.
Because
\widetilde{\iota\circ f}
extends
\iota\circ f
, we have containment

C_{0}~\subset~\widetilde{\iota\circ f}^{-1}(\widetilde{T})~\subset~C

in the preimage of the tubular neighborhood
\widetilde{T}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
of
\iota(T)
.
Using that
C_{0}\subset C
is properly acyclic, choose a smooth proper embedding
C\xrightarrow{e}C
extending the inclusion
C_{0}\hookrightarrow C
whose image is contained in
\widetilde{\iota\circ f}^{-1}(\widetilde{T})
.
Consider the composite map

\widetilde{f}\colon C\xrightarrow{~e~}\widetilde{\iota\circ f}^{-1}(\widetilde{T})\xrightarrow{~\widetilde{\iota\circ f}~}\widetilde{T}\xrightarrow{~\pi~}T~.

This map is a composition of smooth maps, and therefore it is smooth.
Furthermore, this map
\widetilde{f}
extends
f
because
\widetilde{\iota\circ f}
extends
\iota\circ f
, and because
\pi
is a retraction.

∎

Lemma 2.3.4
.

Consider a solid diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Th(\xi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}

in which
\xi=(E\to B)
is a smooth rank-
k
vector bundle,
C
is a manifold with corners,
C_{0}\subset C
is a properly acyclic closed union of faces, and
f
is a compactly-supported map that restricts to the closure of each face in
C_{0}
as a smooth compactly-supported map.

(1)

There is a smooth compactly-supported extension
\widetilde{f}
.

(2)

If the restriction of
f
along each face
F
in
C_{0}
is transverse to the zero-section,
f_{|F}\pitchfork B
, then there exists a smooth compactly-supported extension
\widetilde{f}
that is also transverse to the zero-section,
\widetilde{f}\pitchfork B
.

Proof.

These statements readily reduces to the case in which
C
is connected.
We now reduce these statements to the case in which
B
is connected.
Write
B=\underset{\alpha}{\coprod}B_{\alpha}
as a coproduct of its connected components.
The Thom space
\Th(\xi)\cong\underset{\alpha}{\bigvee}\Th(\xi_{|B_{\alpha}})

is canonically homeomorphic to the wedge sum of the Thom spaces of the base-change of
\xi
over each connected component of
B
.
So the map
f
is a
\pi_{0}(B)
-indexed sequence
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\pi_{0}(B)}
of compactly-supported maps with mutually disjoint supports:
{\sf Supp}(f_{\alpha})\cap{\sf Supp}(f_{\beta})=\emptyset
for
\alpha\neq\beta
.
Note the identity
{\sf Supp}(f)=\underset{\alpha\in\pi_{0}(B)}{\bigcup}{\sf Supp}(f_{\alpha})
between subspaces of
C
.
The assumption that
f
is compactly-supported implies the set
\{\alpha\in\pi_{0}(B)\mid{\sf Supp}(f_{\alpha})\neq\emptyset\}
is finite.
Using that
C
is normal, for each
\alpha\in\pi_{0}(B)
such that
{\sf Supp}(f_{\alpha})\neq\emptyset
, choose an open neighborhood
{\sf Supp}(f_{\alpha})\subset U_{\alpha}\subset C
with compact closure such that
U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}=\emptyset
for
\alpha\neq\beta
.
Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\alpha}\cap C_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Th(\xi_{|B_{\alpha}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}_{\alpha}}

be a smooth extension as in statement (1) in the case that
B
is connected.
The map

C\xrightarrow{~\widetilde{f}~}\underset{\alpha\in\pi_{0}(B)}{\bigvee}\Th(\xi_{|B_{\alpha}})\cong\Th(\xi)~,\qquad c\longmapsto\begin{cases}\widetilde{f}_{\alpha}(c)&,~\text{ if }c\in U_{\alpha}\\
+&,~\text{ if }c\notin\underset{\alpha}{\bigcup}U_{\alpha}\end{cases}~,

is a smooth extension as in statement (1).
Clearly, if
\widetilde{f}_{\alpha}
is transverse to
B_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha
, then
\widetilde{f}
is transverse to
B
.
This completes the reduction to the case in which
B
is connected.

In what follows, we assume both
C
and
B
are connected.
In particular, both
C
and
B
have constant dimension.
Using this, choose
N>{\sf dim}(C)/2
together with a smooth embedding
\xi\hookrightarrow\epsilon^{N}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}
into a trivial rank-
N
vector bundle over
\mathbb{R}^{N}
such that the smooth embedding between bases
B\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
has compact closure.
This embedding uniquely extends as a pointed embedding
\Th(\xi)\xrightarrow{\iota}\Th(\epsilon^{N}_{\mathbb{R}^{N}})\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{2N})^{+}
to the one-point compactification of
\mathbb{R}^{2N}
, such that restriction
E\xrightarrow{\iota_{|}}\mathbb{R}^{2N}
is smooth.

Because
f
is continuous and compactly-supported, the preimage
f^{-1}(E)=(\iota\circ f)^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2N})\subset C_{0}
is an open subset whose closure is compact.
It follows that the smooth map
f^{-1}(E)\xrightarrow{f_{|}}E\xrightarrow{\iota_{|}}\mathbb{R}^{2N}
is proper.
Using that
C
is locally compact, choose an open subset
\widetilde{f}^{-1}(E)\subset C
whose closure is compact and such that
\widetilde{f}^{-1}(E)\cap C_{0}=f^{-1}(E)
.
Note that
f^{-1}(E)\subset\widetilde{f}^{-1}(E)
is a closed union of faces in a manifold with corners; in particular, the inclusion
f^{-1}(E)\hookrightarrow\widetilde{f}^{-1}(E)
is a proper map.
Using Lemma
2.3.3
(2), choose a proper smooth extension:

\hbox to159.61pt{\vbox to56.58pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 79.80618pt\lower-27.09561pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.2271pt}{-9.29922pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

Define

\widetilde{\iota\circ f}\colon C\xrightarrow{~c~}(\widetilde{f}^{-1}(E))^{+}\xrightarrow{~(\widetilde{\iota_{|}\circ f_{|}})^{+}~}(\mathbb{R}^{2N})^{+}~,

where
c
is the collapse map of
C
onto the one-point compactification of its open subspace
\widetilde{f}^{-1}(E)\subset C
.
This map is a smooth compactly-supported map because it is a composition of such.
Commutativity of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Th(\xi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\mathbb{R}^{2N})^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(f^{-1}(E))^{+}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\widetilde{f}^{-1}(E))^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{|}\circ f_{|})^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{\iota_{|}\circ f_{|}})^{+}}

ensures this map
\widetilde{\iota\circ f}
extends
\iota\circ f
.

Using the tubular neighborhood theorem applied to
\iota(E)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2N}
, together with the complement of a large open ball about the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{2N}
, choose the following.

•

An open neighborhood
\iota(\Th(\xi))\subset U\subset(\mathbb{R}^{2N})^{+}
.

•

A deformation retraction
[0,1]\times U\xrightarrow{\rho}U
of
U
onto
\iota(\Th(\xi))
such that the restriction
[0,1]\times\rho_{1}^{-1}(\iota(E))\xrightarrow{\rho_{|}}\rho_{1}^{-1}(\iota(E))
is a smooth deformation retraction of the preimage
\rho_{1}^{-1}(\iota(E))
onto
\iota(E)
. (So,
\rho_{0}=\id_{U}
and
\rho_{1}
is a retraction.)

Using that
\widetilde{\iota\circ f}
is continuous, and that
\iota\circ f(C_{0})\subset\iota(\Th(\xi))
, the preimage

C_{0}~\subset~\widetilde{\iota\circ f}^{-1}(U)~\subset~C

is an open neighborhood of
C_{0}
.
Using that
U
contains an open neighborhood of
+\in(\mathbb{R}^{2N})^{+}
, properness of
\widetilde{\iota\circ f}
ensures this open subset
\widetilde{\iota\circ f}^{-1}(U)\subset C
has compact complement.
Using that
C_{0}\subset C
is properly acyclic, choose a smooth proper embedding
C\xrightarrow{e}C
extending the inclusion
C_{0}\hookrightarrow C
and such that there is containment of its image:
e(C)\subset\widetilde{\iota\circ f}^{-1}(U)
.
The composite map

\widetilde{f}\colon C\xrightarrow{~e~}\widetilde{\iota\circ f}^{-1}(U)\xrightarrow{~\widetilde{\iota\circ f}_{|}~}U\xrightarrow{~\rho_{1}~}\iota(\Th(\xi))\xrightarrow{~\iota^{-1}~}\Th(\xi)

is well-defined and continuous and it extends
f
.
Because
e
and
\widetilde{\iota\circ f}
are compactly-supported and smooth, and
\rho_{1}
is smooth and
\iota
is a smooth embedding, it follows that
\widetilde{f}
is compactly-supported and smooth.
This completes the proof of statement (1).

Now suppose the restriction of
f
along each face in
C_{0}
is transverse to the zero-section,
f\pitchfork B
.
Using statement (1), choose a smooth compactly-supported extension
\widetilde{f}
.
Using that
f
is compactly-supported,
and using openness of transversality (e.g., Theorem 2.1 of
[
Hi
]
),
choose an open neighborhood
C_{0}\subset V\subset C
such that
V\xrightarrow{\widetilde{f}_{|V}}\Th(\xi)
is transverse to the zero-section,
\widetilde{f}_{|V}\pitchfork B
.
Using that
C_{0}\subset C
is properly acyclic, choose a smooth proper embedding
C\xrightarrow{e}C
extending the inclusion
C_{0}\hookrightarrow C
and such that there is containment of the image
e(C)\subset V
.
Then
\widetilde{f}\circ e
is a smooth compactly-supported extension of
f
that is transverse to the zero-section
B\subset E
.

∎

2.4.
The Kan condition for smooth mapping spaces

We extend a simplicial object
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{X}
in an
\infty
-category with limits to a functor
\sSet^{\op}\xrightarrow{j_{\ast}F}\mathcal{X}
via right Kan extension along the Yoneda embedding
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}\xrightarrow{j}\sSet^{\op}
.
Explicitly, this extension evaluates on
S\in\sSet
as a limit:

(3)

F(S)~:=~(j_{\ast}F)(S)~=~\underset{([p]\rightarrow S)^{\circ}\in(\mathbf{\Delta}_{/S})^{\op}}{\limit}F([p])~.

In the case that
\mathcal{X}=\Spaces
, recognize this value as the space of morphisms
F(S)=\Hom_{\PShv(\mathbf{\Delta})}(S,F)
to
F
from
S
regarded as a simplicial space.

Definition 2.4.1
.

A simplicial space
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}\xrightarrow{F}\Spaces

satisfies the Kan condition
if for each
p\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq p
, the canonical map

F([p])\longrightarrow F(\Lambda_{i}[p])

is surjective on path-components.

We prove the following technical result at the end of this subsection, after some set-up.

Lemma 2.4.2
.

All of the simplicial spaces
\cMap_{\bullet}(M,\Th(\xi))
and
\cMap^{\sf sm}_{\bullet}(M,\Th(\xi))

and

\cMap^{\pitchfork}_{\bullet}(M,\Th(\xi))

satisfy the Kan condition.

Let
S
be a simplicial set.
The
subdivision
of
S
is the full subcategory

\sd(S)~\subset~\mathbf{\Delta}_{/S}

consisting of those objects
([p]\in\mathbf{\Delta},[p]\xrightarrow{s}S)
such that
s
is not degenerate.

Observation 2.4.3
.

If
S
is a simplicial subset of a representable simplicial set, then the category
\sd(S)
is a finite poset.

Observation 2.4.4
.

Let
S
be a simplicial set.
Via the surjective-injective factorization system on
\mathbf{\Delta}
,
the canonical inclusion

\sd(S)~\hookrightarrow~\mathbf{\Delta}_{/S}

is a right adjoint, and is therefore final.
Consequently, for
\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{X}
a simplicial object in an
\infty
-category with limits, the canonical morphism

F(S)\xrightarrow{~\simeq~}\underset{([p]\rightarrow S)^{\circ}\in\sd(S)^{\op}}{\limit}F([p])

is an equivalence.

Temporarily denote the underlying space functor
U\colon\Top\rightarrow\Spaces
.
Let
F\colon\mathbf{\Delta}^{\op}\rightarrow\Top
be a simplicial topological space, such as
\cMap^{\pitchfork}_{\bullet}(M,\Th(\xi))
.
Let
S\in\sSet
be a simplicial set.
Using that homotopy limits in
\Top
present limits in
\Spaces
(Theorem 4.2.4.1 of
[
Lu1
]
), the canonical map between spaces

U\bigl(F(S)\bigr)\longrightarrow(UF)(S)

may be identified as
U
applied to the canonical map between topological spaces

(4)

\underset{([p]\rightarrow S)^{\circ}\in\sd(S)^{\op}}{\sf lim}F([p])\longrightarrow\underset{([p]\rightarrow S)^{\circ}\in\sd(S)^{\op}}{\sf holim}F([p])

from the point-set limit to the homotopy limit (in the sense of Bousfield–Kan
[
BK
]
).
In the cases
F=\cMap_{\bullet}(M,\Th(\xi))
and
F=\cMap^{\sf sm}_{\bullet}(M,\Th(\xi))
and
F=\cMap^{\pitchfork}_{\bullet}(M,\Th(\xi))
, let us respectively denote the map (
4
) as

(5)

\displaystyle\cMap(|S|\times M,\Th(\xi))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\cMap_{S}(M,\Th(\xi))~,

(6)

\displaystyle\cMap^{\sm}(|S|\times M,\Th(\xi))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\cMap_{S}^{\sm}(M,\Th(\xi))~,

(7)

\displaystyle\cMap^{\pitchfork}(|S|\times M,\Th(\xi))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\cMap_{S}^{\pitchfork}(M,\Th(\xi))~.

We prove the following result later in this subsection, after some setup.

Lemma 2.4.5
.

For
0\leq i\leq p
, let
S
be either of the simplicial sets
\Lambda_{i}[p]
or
\partial[p]
.
In these cases, the canonical maps (
5
), (
6
), and (
7
) are weak homotopy equivalences.

Let us explicate the points of the domains and codomains of the maps (
5
), (
6
), and (
7
).
As the notation suggests, a point in the domain of (
5
) is a compactly-supported map

|S|\times M\xrightarrow{~f~}\Th(\xi)

from the product of
M
with the geometric realization of
S
.
A point in the domain of (
6
) is such a compactly-supported map that is
smooth
, meaning, for each non-degenerate simplex
[p]\xrightarrow{\sigma}S
, the composite compactly-supported map
\Delta^{p}\times M\xrightarrow{|\sigma|\times M}|S|\times M\xrightarrow{f}\Th(\xi)
is smooth in the sense of Definition
2.1.2
.
A point in the domain of (
7
) is such a smooth compactly-supported map that is
transverse to the zero-section
, meaning, for each non-degenerate simplex
[p]\xrightarrow{\sigma}S
, the composite smooth compactly-supported map
\Delta^{p}\times M\xrightarrow{|\sigma|\times M}|S|\times M\xrightarrow{f}\Th(\xi)
is transverse to
B\subset\Th(\xi)
in the sense of Definition
2.1.4
.

We now explicate points in the codomains of the maps (
5
), (
6
), and (
7
).
Observation
2.4.4
implies the geometric realization of
S
may be expressed as the colimit

|S|\xleftarrow{~\cong~}\colim\Bigl(\sd(S)\xrightarrow{\rm forget}\mathbf{\Delta}\xrightarrow{\Delta^{\bullet}}\Top\Bigr)~=~\underset{([p]\xrightarrow{\sigma}S)\in\sd(S)}{\colim}\Delta^{p}~.

Consider the
homotopy geometric realization
, by which we mean the homotopy colimit (in the sense of Bousfield–Kan
[
BK
]
):

|S|_{\sf h}~:=~\hocolim\Bigl(\sd(S)\xrightarrow{\rm forget}\mathbf{\Delta}\xrightarrow{\Delta^{\bullet}}\Top\Bigr)~=~\underset{([p]\xrightarrow{\sigma}S)\in\sd(S)}{\hocolim}\Delta^{p}~=~\underset{([q]\xrightarrow{\rho}[p]\xrightarrow{\sigma}S)\in\TwAr(\sd(S))}{\colim}|\sd(S)^{\sigma\rho/}|\times\Delta^{p}~,

which is, again, the geometric realization of a finite simplicial set.

There is a canonical map

(8)

|S|_{\sf h}\longrightarrow|S|~,

which is a quotient map by collapsing some simplices in
|S|_{\sf h}
.
Because the diagram
\sd(S)\xrightarrow{\rm forget}\mathbf{\Delta}\xrightarrow{\Delta^{\bullet}}\Top

is cofibrant, this quotient map is a homotopy equivalence.
Unpacking the Bousfield–Kan definition of homotopy limits and homotopy colimits reveals an identification between topological spaces

\cMap_{S}(M,\Th(\xi))\xrightarrow{~\simeq~}\underset{([p]\xrightarrow{\sigma}S)^{\circ}\in\sd(S)^{\op}}{\holim}\cMap(\Delta^{p}\times M,\Th(\xi))~=~\cMap\Bigl(|S|_{\sf h}\times M,\Th(\xi)\Bigr)~.

So a point in the codomain of (
5
) is a compactly-supported map

|S|_{\sf h}\times M\xrightarrow{~f~}\Th(\xi)~.

A point in the codomain of (
6
) is, in particular, such a compactly-supported map that is
partially smooth
, meaning, for each
(\sigma\rho\xrightarrow{\rho}\sigma)\in\TwAr(\sd(S))
, the composite compactly-supported map
|\sd(S)^{\sigma\rho/}|\times\Delta^{p}\times M\hookrightarrow|S|_{\sf h}\times M\xrightarrow{f}\Th(\xi)
is a
|\sd(S)^{\sigma\rho/}|
-family of smooth compactly-supported maps
\Delta^{p}\times M\to\Th(\xi)
in the sense of Definition
2.1.2
.
A point in the codomain of (
7
) is, in particular, such a compactly-supported map that is
partially smooth and transverse
, meaning, for each
(\sigma\rho\xrightarrow{\rho}\sigma)\in\TwAr(\sd(S))
, the composite compactly-supported map
|\sd(S)^{\sigma\rho/}|\times\Delta^{p}\times M\hookrightarrow|S|_{\sf h}\times M\xrightarrow{f}\Th(\xi)
is a
|\sd(S)^{\sigma\rho/}|
-family of smooth compactly-supported maps
\Delta^{p}\times M\to\Th(\xi)
that are transverse to the zero-section
B\subset\Th(\xi)
in the sense of Definition
2.1.4
.

Proof of Lemma
2.4.5
.

We prove the relative homotopy groups of each of the maps (
5
), (
6
), and (
7
) is trivial, starting with the case of the map (
5
).
So let
q\geq 0
and consider a pair
(f,\widetilde{f}_{0})
of horizontal maps fitting into a commutative diagram:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

Consider the subspace

C_{0}~:=~\Bigl(\mathbb{S}^{q-1}\times|S|\underset{\mathbb{S}^{q-1}\times|S|_{\sf h}}{\coprod}\mathbb{D}^{q}\times|S|_{\sf h}\Bigr)\times M~\subset~C

Observe that the maps
f
and
\widetilde{f}_{0}
, together, define a compactly-supported map from the pushout

C_{0}~=~\Bigl(\mathbb{S}^{q-1}\times|S|\underset{\mathbb{S}^{q-1}\times|S|_{\sf h}}{\coprod}\mathbb{D}^{q}\times|S|_{\sf h}\Bigr)\times M\xrightarrow{~f~}\Th(\xi)~;

because the left vertical map in the above diagram is a quotient map, the map
\widetilde{f}_{0}
is determined by
f
, thereby justifying simply denoting the above map as
f
.

Now consider the mapping cylinder

{\sf Cyl}\Bigl(|S|_{\sf h}\xrightarrow{(\ref{e110})}|S|\Bigr)~.

Consider the topological space

C~:=~\Bigl(\mathbb{S}^{q-1}\times I\times|S|\underset{\mathbb{S}^{q-1}\times{\sf Cyl}\Bigl(|S|_{\sf h}\xrightarrow{(\ref{e110})}|S|\Bigr)}{\coprod}\mathbb{D}^{q}\times{\sf Cyl}\Bigl(|S|_{\sf h}\xrightarrow{(\ref{e110})}|S|\Bigr)\Bigr)\times M~,

which is the product of
M
with a pushout.
Observe a canonical inclusion
C_{0}\subset C
.
Finiteness of
S
implies the pushout factor of
C
is compact.
Using that the quotient map
|S|_{\sf h}\xrightarrow{(\ref{e110})}|S|
is a homotopy equivalence given by collapsing simplices, this inclusion
|S|_{\sf h}\hookrightarrow{\sf Cyl}\Bigl(|S|_{\sf h}\xrightarrow{(\ref{e110})}|S|\Bigr)
is an acyclic.
Consequently, the inclusion
C_{0}\hookrightarrow C
is a properly acyclic cofibration between topological spaces.
Therefore, there is an extension of
f
to a compactly-supported map

\widetilde{f}\colon C\longrightarrow\Th(\xi)~.

The restriction to one end of the cylinder,

\widetilde{f}_{|}\colon\mathbb{D}^{q}\times|S|\times M\hookrightarrow C\longrightarrow\Th(\xi)~,

is then a compactly-supported map.
Furthermore,
\widetilde{f}
witnesses a homotopy between
f
and
\widetilde{f}_{|}
as well as a homotopy between
\widetilde{f}_{0}
and the restriction of
\widetilde{f}_{|}
along
\mathbb{S}^{q-1}\times|S|\times M\hookrightarrow\mathbb{D}^{q-1}\times|S|\times M
.
Therefore, this map
\widetilde{f}_{|}
is the sought homotopy filler in the diagram (
9
).
We conclude that the map (
5
) is a weak homotopy equivalence, as desired.

We now prove that the relative homotopy groups of the map (
6
) are trivial.
So consider a diagram

(10)

We now use the assumption that
S=\partial[p]
or
\Lambda_{i}[p]
for some
0\leq i\leq p
.
In these cases, this topological space
C
has a canonical structure of a compact manifold with corners such that the canonical inclusion
C_{0}\hookrightarrow C
is a properly acyclic closed union of faces.
By proper smooth approximation (e.g., as follows from
[
KM
]
), homotope the pair
(f,\widetilde{f}_{0})
to one for which the continuous map
C_{0}\xrightarrow{f}\Th(\xi)
is a smooth compactly-supported map (i.e., its restriction to each face in
C_{0}
is a smooth compactly-supported map).
By Lemma
2.3.4
(1), there exists a smooth compactly-supported extension

\widetilde{f}\colon C\longrightarrow\Th(\xi)

of
f
.
Its restriction

\widetilde{f}_{|}\colon\mathbb{D}^{q}\times|S|\times M\hookrightarrow C\longrightarrow\Th(\xi)

is then a smooth compactly-supported map.
Furthermore,
\widetilde{f}
witnesses a smooth homotopy between
f
and
\widetilde{f}_{|}
as well as a smooth homotopy between
\widetilde{f}_{0}
and the restriction of
\widetilde{f}_{|}
along
\mathbb{S}^{q-1}\times|S|\times M
.
Therefore, this map
\widetilde{f}_{|}
is the sought homotopy filler in the diagram (
10
).
We conclude that the map (
6
) is a weak homotopy equivalence, as desired.

Lastly, we prove that the relative homotopy groups of the map (
7
) are trivial.
So consider a diagram

(11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-36.13051pt}{-25.82475pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
